Q. 
Please describe your educational background and professional 
1 
experience. 
2 
A. 
I graduated from the Massachusetts Institute of Technology in 1978 with a 
3 
Bachelor of Science in Geology.  From 1981 to 1989, I worked as a 
4 
Research Associate with Jensen Associates, Inc. of Boston where I was 
5 
responsible for developing a variety of computer forecasting models to 
6 
analyze natural gas supply and demand for interstate pipeline and local 
7 
distribution companies.  Upon accepting employment with Boston Gas 
8 
Company in 1989, I was responsible for modeling and forecasting the 
9 
natural gas resource requirements of the company’s customers and 
10 
managing its resource-planning process.  In 1998 and 1999, I undertook the 
11 
same responsibility for Essex Gas Company and Colonial Gas Company, 
12 
respectively.  In 2000, I assumed responsibility for modeling and 
13 
forecasting the natural gas resource requirements of KEDNY and KEDLI.  
14 
In 2008, I assumed the additional responsibility for modeling and 
15 
forecasting the natural gas resource requirements of The Narragansett 
16 
Electric Company and Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation (“NMPC”). 
17 
 
18

II. 
Purpose of Testimony 
5 
Q. 
What is the purpose of your testimony? 
6 
A. 
The purpose of our testimony is to provide historic and forecast customer 
7 
demand data used by the Companies to prepare the forecast of gas 
8 
revenues and gas costs for the twelve months ending March 31, 2025 
9 
(“Rate Year”).  The data includes historic data through October 2022 and 
10 
forecast data up to and including the Rate Year and the following three 
11 
years ending March 31, 2026, March 31, 2027, and March 31, 2028 (“Data 
12 
Year 1,” “Data Year 2,” and “Data Year 3,” respectively).  The Companies 
13 
are including forecast data for Data Year 1, Data Year 2, and Data Year 3 
14 
(collectively, the “Data Years”) for informational purposes and to facilitate 
15 
the possibility of a multi-year rate settlement in this proceeding.  For 
16 
purposes of this testimony, Fiscal Year (“FY”) shall mean the Companies’ 
17 
fiscal year, which is the twelve-month period from April 1st to March 31st. 
18 
 
19 
 
20 
 
21

III. 
Overview of the Companies’ Retail Gas Deliveries 
10 
Q. 
Please provide a summary of the Companies’ forecast over the FY 2023 
11 
through FY 2028 time period. 
12 
A. 
KEDNY’s natural gas deliveries are projected to increase by 10.1 million 
13 
therms per year or a compound annual growth rate of 0.60 percent per 
14 
annum.  Net meter counts are projected to increase by 2,969 meters per 
15 
year or a compound annual growth rate of 0.23 percent per annum.  
16 
 
17 
 
KEDLI’s natural gas deliveries are projected to increase by 15.3 million 
18 
therms per year or a compound annual growth rate of 1.37 percent per 
19 
annum.  Net meter counts are projected to increase by 6,562 meters per 
20 
year or a compound annual growth rate of 1.02 percent per annum. 
21 


--- Page 14 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 12 of 58 
The Companies’ forecast growth is net of projected savings from demand-
1 
side management programs in the Companies’ service areas and the impact 
2 
of local laws intended to reduce gas usage.  The specific methodology used 
3 
for the Companies’ forecasts is described in further detail below.  
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Please provide an overview of the Companies’ historic gas deliveries 
6 
from FY 2018 through FY 2022. 
7 
A. 
For KEDNY, actual retail gas deliveries have ranged from 1.530 billion 
8 
therms in FY 2022 to 1.716 billion therms in FY 2019 to all customers, 
9 
excluding non-core (SC 18-5A Non-Core Transport) and electric 
10 
generation (SC 18 Small Electric Generator Transport and SC 20 Large 
11 
Electric Generator Transport) (Exhibit ___ (GLF-1A)).  “Retail” refers to 
12 
gas delivered and metered at the customers’ premises, while “wholesale” 
13 
refers to gas received and metered flowing into the Companies’ 
14 
distribution systems.  The range in volumes of actual retail gas deliveries 
15 
was primarily driven by fluctuations in the weather, such as the colder-
16 
than-normal FY 2019 and the warmer-than-normal FY 2020 and FY 2022.  
17 
On a weather-normalized basis, KEDNY’s retail gas deliveries to all 
18 
customers, excluding non-core and electric generation, have grown at a 
19 
compound annual growth rate of 0.02 percent per annum from 1.606 
20 
billion therms in FY 2018 to 1.607 billion therms in FY 2022 (Exhibit ___ 
21 


--- Page 15 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 13 of 58 
(GLF-8A)).  This growth rate reflects the impacts of the COVID-19 
1 
pandemic, the current economy, and the volatility of natural gas prices.  
2 
KEDNY’s historic actual billed sales by month and by class for FY 2018 
3 
– FY 2022 are provided in Exhibit ___ (GLF-7A).  KEDNY’s historic 
4 
weather-normalized billed sales by month and by class for FY 2018 – FY 
5 
2022 are provided in Exhibit ___ (GLF-8A). 
6 
 
7 
For KEDLI, actual retail gas deliveries have ranged from 0.985 billion 
8 
therms to 1.073 billion therms to all customers, excluding small and large 
9 
electric generation (SC 7 Small Electric Generator Transport and SC 14 
10 
Large Electric Generator Transport) (Exhibit ___ (GLF-1B)).  Similar to 
11 
KEDNY, the range in volumes of actual retail gas deliveries for KEDLI 
12 
was primarily driven by weather fluctuations.  On a weather-normalized 
13 
basis, KEDLI’s retail gas deliveries to all customers excluding non-core 
14 
and electric generation have grown at a compound annual growth rate of 
15 
1.2 percent per annum from 1.008 billion therms in FY 2018 to 1.056 
16 
billion therms in FY 2022 (Exhibit ___ (GLF-8B)).  Similar to KEDNY, 
17 
this growth rate reflects recovery from the impacts of the COVID-19 
18 
pandemic, the economy, and the volatility of natural gas prices.  KEDLI’s 
19 
historic actual billed sales by month and by class for FY 2018 – FY 2022 
20 
are provided in Exhibit ___ (GLF-7B).  KEDLI’s historic weather-
21

normalized billed sales by month and by class for FY 2018 – FY 2022 are 
1 
provided in Exhibit ___ (GLF-8B).

IV. 
Gas Delivery Forecast 
1 
Q. 
Please describe KEDNY’s individual service and revenue class 
2 
distinctions. 
3 
A. 
KEDNY’s gas tariff (PSC No. 12) includes the following active service 
4 
classifications: SC 1, SC 2, SC 3, SC 4, SC 7, SC 17, SC 18, SC 20, SC 
5 
21, and SC22.  Certain service classifications contain distinct revenue 
6 
classes.  The revenue classes include Residential Non-Heat, Residential 
7 
Heat, Commercial Non-Heat, Commercial Heat, and Industrial classes. 
8 
Powerplants and cogeneration facilities (SC 18 and SC 20) are not 
9 
included in the sales forecast.    
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Please describe KEDLI’s individual service and revenue class 
12 
distinctions. 
13 
A.  
KEDLI’s gas tariff (PSC No. 1) includes the following active service 
14 
classifications: SC 1, SC 2, SC 3, SC 5, SC 7, SC 9, SC 14, SC 15, SC 16, 
15 
SC 17, SC 18, and SC 19.  Certain service classifications contain distinct 
16 
revenue classes.  The revenue classes include Residential Non-Heat, 
17 
Residential Heat, Commercial Non-Heat, Commercial Heat, and Industrial 
18 
classes.  Powerplants and cogeneration facilities (SC 7 and SC 14) are not 
19 
included in the sales forecast.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 16 of 58 
Q. 
Please describe how you derived the delivery forecasts for KEDNY 
1 
and KEDLI. 
2 
A. 
On an annual basis, the Companies prepare their gas load forecasts based 
3 
on econometric/statistical forecast models developed for residential, 
4 
commercial/industrial, and multi-family rate groups.  The instant forecasts 
5 
were based on historical billing data from March 2007 through October 
6 
2022.   
7 
 
8 
Although the Companies maintain their customer data at the internal rate 
9 
code level (48 unique rate codes for KEDNY and 39 unique rate codes for 
10 
KEDLI), the data from these rate codes were aggregated into six rate 
11 
groups: Residential Heating, Residential Non-Heating, Commercial, 
12 
Multi-Family, Non-Firm Demand Response, and Other.  By aggregating 
13 
its data into these six groups, the Companies developed population sets 
14 
that were large enough for statistical modeling as well as free of variations 
15 
that are not driven by economic factors.  The tables below summarize the 
16 
service classifications that comprised the rate groups: 
17 
 
18 
 
19 
 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 19 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 17 of 58 
KEDNY Rate Groups 
Rate Group 
Service Classifications 
Residential Heating 
SC 1B and SC 17-1B 
Residential Non-Heating 
SC 1A and SC 17-1A 
Commercial 
SC 2-1, SC 2-2, SC 17-2-1, and 
SC 17-2-2 
Multi-Family 
SC 3 and SC 17-3 
Non-Firm Demand Response 
SC 22 and SC 18-22 
Other 
Other 
 
1 
KEDLI Rate Groups 
Rate Group 
Service Classifications 
Residential Heating 
SC 1B and SC 5-1B 
Residential Non-Heating 
SC 1A and SC 5-1A 
Commercial 
SC 2A, SC 2B, SC 5-2A, and SC 
5-2B 
Multi-Family 
SC 3 and SC 5-3 
Non-Firm Demand Response 
SC 18 and SC 19 
Other 
Other 
 
2 
Two different models were developed for each rate group: (i) a model to 
3 
forecast the number of active, flowing meters that are generating bills, and 
4 
(ii) a model to forecast use-per-meter.  The Companies’ volume forecasts 
5 
are then the product of number of meters multiplied by the use-per-meter 
6 
for each rate group.  The meter count and use-per-meter models are 
7 
developed using linear regression techniques and are selected based on 
8 
econometric best practices (i.e. sign and significance of independent 
9 
variables and the adjusted R-squared of the models after accounting for 
10 
autocorrelation and heteroscedasticity).   The rate group results were then 
11 


--- Page 20 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 18 of 58 
allocated to the internal rate code level based on the most recent shares of 
1 
the rate codes to their rate group for each month. 
2 
 
3 
In the number of customer (meter count) models, the independent 
4 
variables are a combination of time trends and economic variables, such 
5 
as population, households, employment, and gas and oil prices.  In the 
6 
models calculating use-per-customer, the independent variables are a 
7 
combination of time trends, heating degree days (“HDD”), and economic 
8 
variables, such as population, housing stock, income, employment, 
9 
unemployment, gross domestic product, and gas and oil prices.   
10 
 
11 
KEDNY and KEDLI obtained the historic data and forecasts of the 
12 
independent economic variables from Moody’s, a leading economic 
13 
research and forecasting firm (November 2022).  The Companies obtained 
14 
historical weather data from the WSI Corporation, whose source data is 
15 
from the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (“NOAA”) 
16 
and National Weather Service (“NWS”).  Retail energy price data was 
17 
obtained from the following sources: delivered gas prices were taken from 
18 
Company tariff sheets, state level oil prices are from New York State 
19 
Energy Research and Development Authority (“NYSERDA”) surveys, 
20 
and state level natural gas and electricity prices were obtained from the 
21 


--- Page 21 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 19 of 58 
U.S. Department of Energy, Energy Information Administration (“EIA”).  
1 
The Companies developed the forecast of retail energy prices by escalating 
2 
historical actuals using the EIA’s Short Term Energy Outlook and Annual 
3 
Energy Outlook energy price projections.  As described above, these 
4 
independent variables were used to develop the econometric models and 
5 
generate load forecasts.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please describe how you derived the customer count forecasts.  
8 
A.  
For the meter count (customer count) models, the Companies aggregated 
9 
monthly meter count data, which is available by internal rate code to the 
10 
level of the six rate groups.  Linear regression analyses were then 
11 
performed on the six rate groups for each company to develop the meter 
12 
count models.  After completing the meter count modeling, the results 
13 
were then allocated to the internal rate code level based on the most recent 
14 
ratios of the various internal rate codes to their rate group.  The meter 
15 
counts were then converted to customer counts based on the rate code 
16 
average number of meters per customer. 
17 
 
18 
Summary results of the KEDNY models can be found in Exhibit ___(GLF-
19 
10A).  Summary results of the KEDLI models can be found in Exhibit 
20 


--- Page 22 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 20 of 58 
___(GLF-10C).  The meter count models for each rate group and the data 
1 
used to derive them are described in more detail below.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe how you derived the use-per-customer forecasts. 
4 
A. 
The historic monthly use-per-customer values were obtained by dividing 
5 
the total billed therms for each month by the number of billed customers 
6 
for the month.   
7 
  
8 
For the use-per-customer models, each company aggregated monthly 
9 
volume data, which is available by internal rate code to the level of the six 
10 
rate groups, and then divided the volumes by their respective meter counts.  
11 
Linear regression analyses were then performed on the six rate groups for 
12 
each company to develop its use-per-customer models.  After completing 
13 
use-per-customer modeling, monthly volumes were calculated from the 
14 
product of monthly meter count times monthly use-per-customer.  The 
15 
volume results were then allocated to the internal rate code level based on 
16 
most recent ratios of the various internal rate codes to their rate group. 
17 
 
18 
Summary results of the KEDNY models can be found in Exhibit ___(GLF-
19 
10B).  Summary results of the KEDLI models can be found in Exhibit 
20 


--- Page 23 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 21 of 58 
___(GLF-10D).  The use-per-customer models for each rate group and the 
1 
data used to derive them are described in more detail below.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
How is the weather variable in the models determined? 
4 
A. 
Weather is a fundamental variable in modeling the natural gas 
5 
requirements of the Companies’ customers.  The Companies used gas day 
6 
(10am – 9am) HDD based on temperature observations from the NWS 
7 
Central Park, New York City, New York weather station in the models for 
8 
both KEDNY and KEDLI.  Daily HDD are defined as 65 degrees 
9 
Fahrenheit minus the average daily temperature when the average 
10 
temperature is lower than 65 degrees Fahrenheit; if the temperature is 
11 
higher, the daily HDD is defined as zero.  For the forecasts, as shown in 
12 
Exhibit ___ (GLF-11), the Companies defined their normal HDD based on 
13 
a 30-year average of the period from January 1992 through December 
14 
2021 as 4,544 HDD. 
15 
 
16 
For the Companies’ modeling and forecasting of retail data, the 
17 
Companies calculate billing degree days (“BDD”), which are the sum of 
18 
the daily HDD values from mid-month to mid-month to reflect the lag 
19 
inherent in billing volumes. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 24 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 22 of 58 
Q. 
Do the initial forecasts include the impact of demand-side 
1 
management initiatives and recent changes to local laws/legislation in 
2 
the Companies’ service territories? 
3 
A. 
The Companies’ volume forecasts are based on models of meter count and 
4 
use-per-customer developed for each residential, commercial/industrial, 
5 
and multi-family rate class.  The Companies’ historical sales data includes 
6 
the impact of past demand-side management programs and local laws and 
7 
legislation (discussed below).  The econometric models built from this 
8 
historical data would reflect these past savings in the forecast horizon but 
9 
would not reflect any acceleration in demand-side management initiatives 
10 
or new local laws/legislation expected in next few years.  Therefore, as 
11 
discussed in more detail below, the Companies adjusted the meter count 
12 
and use-per-customer forecasts for funded energy efficiency programs, 
13 
heat electrification programs, water heating electrification, as well as the 
14 
forecast impact of the City of New York Local Laws 154 (“LL154”) and 
15 
97 (“LL97”), which aim to curtail future gas usage (discussed below in 
16 
more detail).   
17 
 
18 
 
19 
 
20 


--- Page 25 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 23 of 58 
Q. 
Why do the Companies forecasts not account for demand response 
1 
programs?    
2 
A. 
Demand response programs in KEDNY and KEDLI aim to curtail usage 
3 
during cold weather and peak periods.  These programs specifically target 
4 
reductions in usage in the morning and early evening hours when demand 
5 
is at its highest.  While these programs are effective at reducing peak gas 
6 
demand during limited demand response events, overall, they have a 
7 
negligible impact on the total monthly usage of enrolled customers.  
8 
Therefore, the Companies consider demand response savings in Design 
9 
Day forecasts, but not in monthly and annual customer usage forecasts.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
How did the Companies adjust the forecasts for sponsored energy 
12 
efficiency programs?  
13 
A. 
As mentioned previously, the Companies’ historical sales data includes the 
14 
impact of actual energy efficiency savings from past company-sponsored 
15 
energy efficiency programs.  Forecast energy efficiency programs are 
16 
reflected in the Companies’ forecasts as a reduction to the base 
17 
econometric forecast when the annual incremental savings exceed the 
18 
average of the most recent three years actual savings to avoid double-
19 
counting of reductions due to energy efficiency. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 26 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 24 of 58 
The annual energy efficiency projections are based on the Companies’ 
1 
expected achievement of New Efficiency New York (“NE:NY”) goals as 
2 
well as additional savings from the Annual Demand Side Management 
3 
(“ADSM”) Filing Update made in March 2022 (Cases 19-G-0309 and 19-
4 
G-0310) through 2025.  These energy efficiency programs are divided into 
5 
two categories: behavioral and persistent.  Behavioral programs, (e.g., 
6 
mailings to inform customers of their energy consumption patterns), are 
7 
programs that require annual contact to maintain the reductions expected 
8 
through outreach.  Persistent programs are those programs expected to 
9 
provide annual reductions once installed throughout the lifetime of the 
10 
measure (e.g., building weatherization improvements). 
11 
 
12 
Because there are no approved programs or goals after 2025, the 
13 
Companies made certain assumptions regarding how levels of energy 
14 
efficiency will continue based on their experience implementing energy 
15 
efficiency programs and the level of energy efficiency saturation by rate 
16 
groups.  Post-2025, KEDNY and KEDLI’s Gross Verified persistent 
17 
savings level (savings that are calculated after accounting for free 
18 
ridership) continues to increase by about one percent until the end of the 
19 
forecast horizon in 2028.  Behavioral program contributions to energy 
20 
efficiency savings through 2025 are based on the expected achievement of 
21 


--- Page 27 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 25 of 58 
NE:NY targets.  Post-2025, behavioral savings decrease year-over-year 
1 
until the end of the forecast horizon in 2028.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
How did the Companies adjust the forecasts based on City of New 
4 
York Local Law 154?  
5 
A. 
LL154 prohibits installation of gas systems or equipment in newly 
6 
constructed buildings in the City of New York less than seven stories tall 
7 
beginning in 2024, and it prohibits such gas use in newly-constructed 
8 
buildings greater than seven stories beginning in 2027 with exceptions for 
9 
certain building types (hospitals, restaurants, commercial kitchens, 
10 
manufacturing, laboratories, laundromats, and power generation).    LL154 
11 
does not restrict or prohibit oil-to-gas conversions in existing buildings or 
12 
low-use upgrades to heating.  
13 
 
14 
Given this information, the Companies estimated the percentage of new 
15 
construction customers in the forecasts that would be affected by LL154 
16 
over the forecast horizon.  By FY 2028 in KEDNY, about 40 to 50 percent 
17 
of the forecast annual meter growth in residential, commercial, and multi-
18 
family from new construction will be affected by LL154.  The Rockaways 
19 
in Queens, New York is the only area impacted by LL154 in KEDLI’s 
20 
service territory, impacting about three percent of annual meter growth 
21 


--- Page 28 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 26 of 58 
from new construction in residential, commercial, and multi-family 
1 
customer groups.  
2 
 
3 
Q.  
How did the Companies adjust their forecasts for New York City 
4 
Local Law 97?  
5 
A. 
LL97 sets greenhouse gas (“GHG”) emission limits on buildings in the 
6 
City of New York over 25,000 square feet beginning in 2024.  Buildings 
7 
must report their total square feet and annual use of electricity, natural gas, 
8 
heating oil, district steam, district hot water, and district chilled water each 
9 
year.  Building emissions intensity is calculated by multiplying the use of 
10 
each of these fuels by their GHG emission factors (building emissions) and 
11 
dividing by building square feet.  If the actual emissions are greater than 
12 
the LL97 emissions limits per square foot, building owners must reduce 
13 
emissions or pay a penalty.  Emissions reduction can be achieved through 
14 
any combination of energy efficiency, fuel switching (oil and steam to gas, 
15 
oil and steam to electric, gas to full and partial electric), and 
16 
decarbonization of fuels.  
17 
 
18 
LL97 annual reporting requirements have been in effect since 2017 so that 
19 
building owners can determine their emissions and take any necessary 
20 
action before the emission limits begin.  The LL97 limits are fixed from 
21 


--- Page 29 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 27 of 58 
2024 to 2029. Compared to the 2019 emission intensity limits, multi-
1 
family and commercial building types, which account for 93 percent of 
2 
total building gas use, are already well below the 2024 to 2029 limits, on 
3 
average. Conversely, the Industrial/Manufacturing building type’s 
4 
emission intensity is more than two times over its 2024 limit.    
5 
 
6 
There are multiple pathways that buildings can choose to achieve the LL97 
7 
emissions limits based on different combinations of measures.  The 
8 
Companies developed a forecast of gas usage by the LL97 buildings based 
9 
on the following assumptions:   
10 
• Continuation of savings from the Companies’ existing and planned 
11 
energy efficiency programs, including electrification; 
12 
• Conversion from oil-to-gas heating in all buildings by 2024; 
13 
• Some gas-to-electric conversions from 2024 to 2030; and 
14 
• About 90 percent reduction in Industrial/Manufacturing gas use by 
15 
2030.   
16 
 
17 
Energy efficiency and electrification savings included in the forecasts 
18 
drive lower gas use for commercial and multi-family customers.  This is 
19 
enough to keep all commercial and multi-family building types on average 
20 
below the LL97 emission limits in 2024 and 2030 onwards.  Because the 
21 


--- Page 30 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 28 of 58 
required reductions in gas use are covered by the energy efficiency and 
1 
electrification projections in the forecast, no adjustment was made to either 
2 
the commercial class or multi-family forecasts to account for LL97. 
3 
 
4 
The Industrial/Manufacturing sector includes power generators and large 
5 
manufacturers on special contracts.  These are very large non-firm gas 
6 
customers that do not participate in utility-sponsored energy efficiency 
7 
programs.  To achieve LL97 compliance, this sector must cut existing gas 
8 
use by about 90 percent by 2030.   These amounts were subtracted from 
9 
“Other” non-firm gas volumes in the forecast.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
How did the Companies adjust the forecasts for sponsored heat 
12 
electrification programs?  
13 
A. 
Because the Companies are not the electric service provider in Downstate 
14 
New York, the electric distribution companies in the KEDNY and KEDLI 
15 
service territories – Consolidated Edison Company of New York, Inc. 
16 
(“Con Edison”) and the Long Island Power Authority (“LIPA”) 
17 
(administered by PSEG Long Island, a subsidiary of Public Service 
18 
Enterprise Group (“PSEG LI”)), provided short-term outlooks on 
19 
electrification projections.  The outlooks were used as a foundation to the 
20 
electrification assumptions utilized in this forecast.  
21 


--- Page 31 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 29 of 58 
The Companies’ electrification forecasts are expressed as heat pump 
1 
targets (i.e., the number of heat pump installations expected to replace gas 
2 
service).  The forecast considers two kinds of heat pump installations: full 
3 
and partial.  Full heat pump installations are sized to meet the customer’s 
4 
full heating requirements and the existing gas service is decommissioned 
5 
when the heat pump is installed.  The forecast treats each full heat pump 
6 
installation as a customer loss, both in terms of customer count and 
7 
associated gas load.   
8 
 
9 
Partial heat pumps are installed with integrated controls connected to the 
10 
existing gas furnace, which remains in service. The forecast assumes that 
11 
these heat pumps operate when outside temperatures are above 30 degrees 
12 
Fahrenheit and switch to the gas system when temperatures are 30 degrees 
13 
Fahrenheit or lower.  Partial heat pumps are not treated as a meter loss in 
14 
the forecast, but they are reclassified as partial heating customers and their 
15 
gas usage is reduced by the amount normally used when temperatures are 
16 
above 30 degrees.   
17 
 
18 
The resulting customer losses from heat electrification programs were 
19 
compared to customer losses expected from LL154 described above to 
20 
avoid double counting.  An adjustment was made only if KEDNY’s 
21 


--- Page 32 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 30 of 58 
customer losses from Con Edison’s electrification programs were greater 
1 
than KEDNY’s customer losses from LL154, as it is assumed that 
2 
structures impacted by LL154 will take advantage of Con Edison’s 
3 
electrification programs.  If the latter was the case, then only the difference 
4 
between the electrification program and LL154 customer losses was 
5 
subtracted from the initial customer count forecasts.  The same 
6 
methodology was applied to KEDLI, where the impact of LL154 is 
7 
minimal.   
8 
 
9 
Q. 
How did the Companies adjust the forecasts for water heating 
10 
electrification?  
11 
A. 
In KEDNY, water heating electrification savings were assumed to be 
12 
captured by Con Edison’s space heating savings projections, so no explicit 
13 
assumptions were made to avoid double counting. 
14 
 
15 
In KEDLI, annual savings due to electrification of water heating were 
16 
obtained by multiplying PSEG LI’s projected number of heat pump water 
17 
heaters (HPWH) by the average dekatherms/HPWH, which are 8.2 per 
18 
year.  These expected annual savings from the HPWH installations were 
19 
subtracted from residential class gas volumes in KEDLI.    
20 
 
21

Q.  
What is the projected impact of demand-side management initiatives 
1 
and local laws on the forecast customer counts in KEDNY?  
2 
A. 
The customer count forecasts reflect the impact of both full electrification 
3 
from Con Edison’s demand-side management programs and LL154.  As 
4 
described above, full electrification occurs when a customer 
5 
decommissions its gas system and installs a heat pump that can meet the 
6 
customer’s entire load.  LL154 prohibits some new construction from 
7 
installing gas systems beginning in 2024, but, as noted above, there are 
8 
several exemptions in the legislation.  A net adjustment was made to 
9 
KEDNY’s customer count for demand-side management electrification 
10 
only if the projected heat pump installations were greater than KEDNY’s 
11 
customer losses from LL154.   
12 
 
13 
By the end of FY 2028, electrification from LL154 and demand side 
14 
management programs decrease the total meter count by 0.8 percent, or 
15 
10,265 meters.  Residential heating customers account for 78.3 percent of 
16 
the total meter loss from electrification (Exhibit ___ (GLF-15A)).

Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel

Q. 
Has the forecast methodology that you described been used previously 
1 
by the Companies? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  Econometric forecasting has been used by KEDLI and KEDNY for 
3 
business, gas resource, and infrastructure planning for years, and was used 
4 
in the 2016 and 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.  The general 
5 
methodology has not changed, but there have been three primary 
6 
enhancements in the Companies’ forecasting process since the 2016 and 
7 
2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases. These enhancements are improved 
8 
model specifications and frequency of input data, the consideration of 
9 
more exogenous factors that impact load, and the addition of market 
10 
saturation limits.  
11 
 
12 
The Companies’ econometric models for meter count and use per customer 
13 
are now based on monthly billing data instead of constructed quarterly 
14 
data.  Furthermore, the peak and off-peak use per customer is no longer 
15 
forecast with separate econometric models.  These two changes to the 
16 
forecast frequency and modelling specification allow for more accurate 
17 
modelling, especially in the peak period.   
18 
 
19 
The Companies also modeled the impact of all known, relevant, and 
20 
quantifiable exogenous factors that could affect load or customer count.  
21 


--- Page 38 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecasting Panel 
Page 36 of 58 
This includes existing or already-filed demand-side management 
1 
programs and public policies that have a clear path to implementation 
2 
during the period of its forecasts.  The relevant factors, described above, 
3 
were expanded from what was considered in the 2016 and 2019 KEDNY 
4 
and KEDLI Rate Cases, to include the impact of energy efficiency, 
5 
electrification initiatives, LL154, and LL97.  
6 
 
7 
The Companies also considered two types of market saturation that limit 
8 
customer growth in certain rate groups.  The first saturation limit is on the 
9 
expected number of residential non-heating to heating conversions.  The 
10 
second saturation assumption imposes restriction on residential, 
11 
commercial, and multi-family growth from off-system conversions.  The 
12 
market saturation limits do not begin to impact customer counts until after 
13 
2030, but were considered for completeness.   
14 
 
15
